\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
and let
r\geq 12mt
. Let
\mathscr{A}
denote the algebra associated with
\mathscr{S}
. Then there exists a measurement
\{G_{c}\}\subset\mathscr{A}
with outcomes in
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
such that

\E_{u\sim[n]^{m}}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\tau\big(G_{c}\,A^{u}_{c(u)}\big)\,\geq\,1-\sigma\;,

where
\sigma=\sigma(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,n^{-1})
with
t=n-d+1
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
from
Lemma

4.4
.

The measurement
\{G_{c}\}
produced by
Lemma

4.4
satisfies
the conclusions of
Theorem

4.1
except it is not projective. To
remedy this we apply the self-improvement lemma to produce a
projective submeasurement, and then complete the result into a
projective measurement.

More formally, let
\{H_{c}\}
be the projective submeasurement
guaranteed by applying
Lemma

4.2
to
G_{c}
. This
submeasurement has the following properties.

1.

(Projectivity):
H_{c}
is projective.

2.

(Completeness): If
H=\sum_{h}H_{h}
, then
\tau(H)\geq 1-\sigma-\zeta
.

3.

(Consistency with
A
): On average over
u\sim[n]^{m}
,
H_{[h\mapsto h(u)\mid a]}\simeq_{\zeta}A^{u}_{a}
.

Let
\tilde{H}
be the measurement obtained by completing

H
arbitrarily, that is by setting
\tilde{H}_{c^{*}}=H_{c^{*}}+\mathbf{1}-\sum_{h}H_{h}
for an arbitrarily chosen outcome
c^{*}
, and setting

\tilde{H}_{c}=H_{c}
for all other outcomes
c\neq c^{*}
. Then

\tilde{H}
has the following properties:

1.

(Projectivity):
\tilde{H}
is projective. This holds by the projectivity of
H
, which implies that

\mathbf{1}-\sum_{h}H_{h}
is a projector and is orthogonal to each

H_{c}
for every outcome
c
.

2.

(Completeness):
\sum_{h}\tilde{H}_{h}=\mathbf{1}
. This holds by
definition.

3.

(Consistency with
A
): We evaluate the (in)consistency between
\tilde{H}
and the points measurements. We have

\displaystyle\E_{u}\sum_{a\neq b}\tau\Big(\tilde{H}_{[h\mapsto h(u)\mid a]}\,A^{u}_{b}\Big)

\displaystyle=\E_{u}\sum_{h,b\neq h(u)}\tau\Big(\tilde{H}_{h}\,A^{u}_{b}\Big)

\displaystyle=\E_{u}\sum_{h,b\neq h(u)}\tau\Big(H_{h}\,A^{u}_{b}\Big)+\tau\Big((\mathbf{1}-\sum_{h}H_{h})\,\sum_{b\neq c^{*}(u)}A^{u}_{b}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\zeta+\tau\Big(\mathbf{1}-\sum_{h}H_{h}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\zeta+\sigma

where in the second-to-last inequality we used that
H
is
\zeta
-consistency with the points measurements
\{A^{u}\}
and that
\sum_{b\neq c^{*}(u)}A^{u}_{b}\leq\mathbf{1}
, and in the last inequality we used that the completeness of
H
is at least
1-\zeta-\sigma
. Thus on average of
u\sim[n]^{m}
, we have

\tilde{H}_{[h\mapsto h(u)\mid a]}\simeq_{2\zeta+\sigma}A^{u}_{a}.

Setting
\delta=\varepsilon
, and
\eta(m,t,r,\varepsilon,n^{-1})=2\zeta(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})+\sigma(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,n^{-1})=\mathrm{poly}(m,t,r)\cdot\Big(\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,n^{-1})+e^{-\Omega(r/m^{2})}\Big)
, we see that
\tilde{H}

satisfies the conclusion of
Theorem

4.1
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.4
.

Define
s_{m}=2m
. We argue that there is a choice of universal constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\geq 1
such that
Lemma

4.4
holds with the function

\sigma(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,n^{-1})=s_{m}\cdot\Big(c_{0}\cdot(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/c_{2}}+\delta^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}})+e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}\Big)\;.

The constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
are set in terms of the universal constants that are implicit in the
\mathrm{poly}(\cdot)
notation for the functions
\zeta,\nu,\mu
from
Lemma

4.2
and
Lemma

4.3
. We now explicitly identify these constants:

\displaystyle\zeta(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})

\displaystyle=z_{0}\cdot(mt)^{z_{1}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/z_{1}}+n^{-1/z_{1}})

(6)

\displaystyle\nu(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,\zeta,n^{-1})

\displaystyle=b_{0}\cdot(mtr)^{b_{1}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/b_{2}}+\delta^{1/b_{2}}+\zeta^{1/b_{2}}+n^{-1/b_{2}})

(7)

\displaystyle\mu(\kappa,m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,\zeta,n^{-1})

\displaystyle=\kappa\Big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\Big)+\nu(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,\zeta,n^{-1})+e^{-\frac{r}{72m^{2}}}

(8)

where
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2},b_{0},b_{1},b_{2}\geq 1
. Now we set the constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
as follows.

1.

Let
c_{0}=4b_{0}z_{0}
.

2.

Let
c_{1}=b_{1}+z_{1}+1
.

3.

Let
c_{2}=b_{2}z_{2}
.

4.

Let
c_{3}=1/72
.

We prove that
Lemma

4.4
holds with this choice of function
\sigma(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,n^{-1})
via induction on
m
, starting with
m=1
.

Base case.

For the case
m=1

there is only one line
\ell=\{(\beta):\beta\in[n]\}
.
This means that the lines measurement
B=\{B^{\ell}\}

contains a single measurement
B^{\ell}
.
Because
\mathscr{S}
passes the tensor codes test with probability
1-\varepsilon
,
it is
(2\varepsilon,4\varepsilon)
-good.
As a result,

B^{\ell}_{[g\mapsto g(u)\mid a]}\simeq_{2\varepsilon}A^{u}_{a}\;.

The theorem follows by setting

G=\{G_{g}\}
equal to
B^{\ell}
, since
c_{0}\geq 2
(which implies that
\sigma\geq 2\varepsilon
).

Induction step.

Assuming, as the inductive hypothesis, that
Lemma

4.4
holds for some
m\geq 1
, we prove it for
m+1
. Let
\mathscr{S}=(\tau,A,B,P)
be a
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-good strategy for the tensor code test with
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes(m+1)}
, and let
r\geq 12(m+1)t
. For each
x\in[n]
,
we define
\mathscr{S}^{x}=(\tau,A^{x},B^{x},P^{x})
to be the strategy restricted on the last coordinate being
x
.
In other words,
we write

\displaystyle A^{x}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{A^{u,x}\}_{u\in[n]^{m}},\quad

B^x

:=

{B^ℓ,x}_ℓ⊂[n]^m,

P^x

:=

{P^u, v,x}_u, v ∈[n]^m,

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad A^{u,x}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle A^{u\circ x},

B^ℓ, x

:=

B^ℓ∘x,

P^u, v, x

:=

P^u ∘x, v ∘x,

where “
\circ
” denotes string concatenation, and
\ell\circ x=\{\alpha\circ x:\alpha\in\ell\}
.
Then
\mathscr{S}^{x}
is a strategy for the
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
tensor code test.
Write
1-\varepsilon_{x}
and
1-\delta_{x}
for the probability that
\mathscr{S}^{x}

passes the axis-parallel lines test and subcube commutation test, respectively.
Then
\mathscr{S}^{x}
is an
(\varepsilon_{x},\delta_{x})
-good strategy.

We claim that on average over
x
chosen uniformly from
[n]
,

\E_{x\sim[n]}\varepsilon_{x}\leq\left(\frac{m+1}{m}\right)\cdot\varepsilon,\;\E_{x\sim[n]}\delta_{x}\leq\left(\frac{m+1}{m}\right)\cdot\delta.

Indeed, observe that in the axis-parallel lines test over
[n]^{m+1}
, the
probability that an axis-parallel line along axis
m+1
is chosen is

1/(m+1)
. Thus,

\varepsilon=\frac{1}{m+1}\cdot\Pr\Big(\text{success for $\ell$ parallel to axis
$m+1$}\Big)+\frac{m}{m+1}\cdot\E_{x}\varepsilon_{x}\geq\frac{m}{m+1}\cdot\E_{x}\varepsilon_{x},

which yields the claimed bound on the expectation of
\varepsilon_{x}
. A
similar calculation holds for the subcube test (the probability that
the subcube does not fix the
m+1
st coordinate is
1/(m+1)
).

Now we apply the inductive hypothesis, noting that
r\geq 12mt
. To do so, fix an
x\in[n]
.
The inductive hypothesis states the existence of a measurement
G^{x}=\{G^{x}_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}

such that on average over
u\in[n]^{m}
,

G^{x}_{[g\mapsto g(u)\mid a]}\simeq_{\sigma_{x}}A^{u,x}_{a},

where
\sigma_{x}=\sigma(m,t,r,\varepsilon_{x},\delta_{x},n^{-1})
.
Setting
\zeta_{x}=\zeta(m,t,\varepsilon_{x},n^{-1})
, self-improvement (
Lemma

4.2
)
then implies the existence of a projective submeasurement
H^{x}=\{H^{x}_{h}\}_{h\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}
with the following properties:

1.

(Completeness): If
H^{x}=\sum_{h}H^{x}_{h}
, then
\tau(H^{x})\geq 1-\sigma_{x}-\zeta_{x}
.

2.

(Consistency with
A^{x}
): On average over
u\sim[n]^{m}
,
H^{x}_{[h\mapsto h(u)\mid a]}\simeq_{\zeta_{x}}A^{u,x}_{a}
.

3.

(Agreement is explained by the complete part of
H^{x}
): There exists a positive linear map
\psi^{x}
such that

\psi^{x}(\mathbf{1}-H^{x})\leq{\zeta_{x}},

and for each
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
and all positive
X\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

\psi^{x}(X)\geq\E_{u\sim[n]^{m}}\tau(X\cdot A^{u,x}_{h(u)}).

These guarantees are almost of the form demanded by the
hypotheses of the pasting lemma (
Lemma

4.3
). However,
the error bounds depend on
x
, whereas we would like the errors
to be averaged over
x
. To obtain such bounds, we will bound the
average of
\sigma_{x},\zeta_{x}
over
x\sim[n]
. Observe that

\sigma_{x}
and
\zeta_{x}
depend on
x
only through
\varepsilon_{x},\delta_{x}\in[0,1]
, and are polynomial functions of
\varepsilon_{x},\delta_{x}
.
For any
distribution over a random variable
\alpha
drawn from the
interval
[0,1]
, for
c\geq 1
, it holds that
\E\alpha^{c}\leq\E\alpha
,
and by concavity, for any
c<1
,
\E\alpha^{c}\leq(\E\alpha)^{c}
. Hence, the averaged quantities
\E_{x}\sigma_{x}
and
\E_{x}\zeta_{x}
, which we denote
\hat{\sigma}
and
\hat{\zeta}
, are polynomial functions of

\E_{x}\varepsilon_{x}\leq\frac{m+1}{m}\varepsilon
and
\E_{x}\delta_{x}\leq\frac{m+1}{m}\delta
:

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}=\E_{x}\sigma_{x}

\displaystyle=s_{m}\cdot c_{0}(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot\E_{x}(\varepsilon_{x}^{1/c_{2}}+\delta_{x}^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}})+s_{m}\cdot e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}

(9)

\displaystyle\leq s_{m}\cdot c_{0}(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot\left(\left(\frac{m+1}{m}\varepsilon\right)^{1/c_{2}}+\left(\frac{m+1}{m}\delta\right)^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}}\right)+s_{m}\cdot e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}

(10)

\displaystyle\leq s_{m}\cdot c_{0}(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot\Big(\frac{m+1}{m}\Big)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}}\cdot\left(\varepsilon^{1/c_{2}}+\delta^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}}\right)+s_{m}\cdot e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}

(11)

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}=\E_{x}\zeta_{x}

\displaystyle=z_{0}(mt)^{z_{1}}\cdot\E_{x}(\varepsilon^{1/z_{2}}+n^{-1/z_{2}})

(12)

\displaystyle\leq z_{0}(mt)^{z_{1}}\cdot\left(\left(\frac{m+1}{m}\varepsilon\right)^{1/z_{2}}+n^{-1/z_{2}}\right)

(13)

\displaystyle\leq z_{0}((m+1)t)^{z_{1}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/z_{2}}+n^{-1/z_{2}})

(14)

Thus we have the following guarantees on the submeasurements
\{H^{x}\}
:

1.

(Completeness): If
H=\E_{x\sim[n]^{m}}\sum_{h}H^{x}_{h}
, then
\tau(H)\geq 1-\hat{\sigma}-\hat{\zeta}
.

2.

(Consistency with
A^{x}
): On average over
(u,x)\sim[n]^{m}\times[n]
,
H^{x}_{[h\mapsto h(u)\mid a]}\simeq_{\hat{\zeta}}A^{u,x}_{a}
.

3.

(Agreement is explained by the complete part of
H^{x}
): For each

x
there exists a positive linear map
\psi^{x}
such that

\E_{x}\psi^{x}(\mathbf{1}-H^{x})\leq{\hat{\zeta}},

and for each
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
and all positive
X\in\mathscr{A}
, we have

\psi(X)\geq\E_{u\sim[n]^{m}}\tau(X\cdot A^{u,x}_{h(u)}).

Define
\hat{\kappa}=\hat{\sigma}+\hat{\zeta}
. We see that the measurements
H^{x}
satisfy the hypotheses of the pasting lemma (
Lemma

4.3
) with error parameters
\hat{\kappa}
and
\hat{\zeta}
. Applying the lemma, we conclude that the resulting measurement
\tilde{H}
satisfies
the following consistency relation with
A
: on average over
(u,x)\in[n]^{m}\times[n]
,

H_{[h\mapsto h(u,x)\mid a]}\simeq_{\mu}A^{u,x}_{a},

where

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\mu(\hat{\kappa},m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,\hat{\zeta},n^{-1})\,=\,\hat{\kappa}\Big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\Big)+\nu(m,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,\hat{\zeta},n^{-1})+e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}

(15)

\displaystyle=\Big(\hat{\sigma}+\hat{\zeta}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\Big)+b_{0}(mtr)^{b_{1}}\cdot\big(\varepsilon^{1/b_{2}}+\delta^{1/b_{2}}+\hat{\zeta}^{1/b_{2}}+n^{-1/b_{2}}\big)+e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}.

(16)

If we show that
\mu\leq\sigma(m+1,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,n^{-1})
, then we will have established the inductive step and we would be done. To proceed, we first substitute (
14
) in for
\hat{\zeta}
. Let
b^{\prime}=b_{2}z_{2}
.

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}^{1/b_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(z_{0}((m+1)t)^{z_{1}})^{1/b_{2}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/z_{2}}+n^{-1/z_{2}})^{1/b_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq z_{0}((m+1)t)^{z_{1}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/b^{\prime}}+n^{-1/b^{\prime}})

where we used that
z_{0}((m+1)t)^{z_{1}}\geq 1
and
b_{2}\geq 1
. Plugging these bounds into (
16
), we get

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)\cdot\big(\hat{\sigma}+\hat{\zeta}\big)+2b_{0}(mtr)^{b_{1}}\cdot z_{0}((m+1)t)^{z_{1}}\cdot\big(\varepsilon^{1/b^{\prime}}+\delta^{1/b^{\prime}}+n^{-1/b^{\prime}}\big)+e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)\cdot\big(\hat{\sigma}+\hat{\zeta}\big)+2b_{0}z_{0}((m+1)tr)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}\big(\varepsilon^{1/b^{\prime}}+\delta^{1/b^{\prime}}+n^{-1/b^{\prime}}\big)+e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}~.

(17)

Similarly, we can upper bound

\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)\cdot\hat{\zeta}\leq 2\cdot z_{0}((m+1)t)^{z_{1}}\cdot(\varepsilon^{1/b^{\prime}}+n^{-1/b^{\prime}})

because, for example,
\varepsilon^{1/b_{2}}\leq\varepsilon^{1/b^{\prime}}
. Thus we get that (
17
) is at most

(
17
)

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)\hat{\sigma}+4b_{0}z_{0}((m+1)tr)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}\big(\varepsilon^{1/b^{\prime}}+\delta^{1/b^{\prime}}+n^{-1/b^{\prime}}\big)+e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}

(18)

Substituting (
11
) in for
\hat{\sigma}
and using that
c_{2}\geq b^{\prime}
we get

(
18
)

\displaystyle\leq\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)s_{m}\cdot c_{0}(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot\Big(\frac{m+1}{m}\Big)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}}\cdot\left(\varepsilon^{1/c_{2}}+\delta^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+4b_{0}z_{0}((m+1)tr)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}\cdot\big(\varepsilon^{1/c_{2}}+\delta^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\Big(\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)s_{m}+1\Big)\cdot e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}~.

(19)

Next, using that
\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)s_{m}\leq s_{m+1}
, we have

(
19
)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(s_{m+1}\cdot c_{0}(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot\Big(\frac{m+1}{m}\Big)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}}+4b_{0}z_{0}((m+1)tr)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}\Big)\cdot\big(\varepsilon^{1/c_{2}}+\delta^{1/c_{2}}+n^{-1/c_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\Big(\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)s_{m}+1\Big)\cdot e^{-c_{3}r/m^{2}}~.

(20)

If we argue both

\displaystyle s_{m+1}\cdot c_{0}(mtr)^{c_{1}}\cdot\Big(\frac{m+1}{m}\Big)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}}+4b_{0}z_{0}((m+1)tr)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}\qquad

\displaystyle\leq\qquad s_{m+1}c_{0}((m+1)tr)^{c_{1}}

(21)

\displaystyle\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)s_{m}+1\qquad

\displaystyle\leq\qquad s_{m+1}

(22)

then we would be done as (
20
) would be at most
\sigma(m+1,t,r,\varepsilon,\delta,n^{-1})
as desired. To argue (
21
) we first note that it is equivalent to showing that

s_{m+1}c_{0}(tr)^{c_{1}}\Big((m+1)^{c_{1}}-m^{c_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}}(m+1)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}}\Big)\geq 4b_{0}z_{0}((m+1)tr)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}~.

Since
s_{m+1}\geq 1
,
c_{0}\geq 4b_{0}z_{0}
by definition, and
c_{1}\geq b_{1}+z_{1}+1
by definition, it suffices to argue that

(m+1)^{c_{1}}-m^{c_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}}(m+1)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}}\geq(m+1)^{b_{1}+z_{1}}~.

This is equivalent to showing

(m+1)^{c_{1}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}-m^{c_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}}(m+1)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}\geq 1~.

Since
b_{1},z_{1},c_{2}\geq 1
it holds that
(m+1)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}\leq m^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}
and thus

\displaystyle(m+1)^{c_{1}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}-m^{c_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}}(m+1)^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}

\displaystyle\geq(m+1)^{c_{1}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}-m^{c_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}}m^{\frac{1}{c_{2}}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}

\displaystyle=(m+1)^{c_{1}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}-m^{c_{1}-(b_{1}+z_{1})}

\displaystyle\geq 1

as desired, where we used that
c_{1}\geq b_{1}+z_{1}+1
again.

To argue (
22
) one can see by definition of
s_{m}
and
s_{m+1}
that

\displaystyle s_{m+1}-\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)s_{m}=2(m+1)-2m\big(1+\frac{1}{3m}\big)\geq 1

as desired.

∎

4.1
Extension to general states

As mentioned in the introduction, Theorem
4.1
is formulated in the framework of synchronous strategies, which is particularly convenient to state and prove the result as it involves a single Hilbert space and set of measurement operators. For some applications one may wish to extend the guarantees of Theorem
4.1
to the more general setting of strategies that are not assumed to be perfectly synchronous a priori. This extension is essentially provided in the work
[
Vid21
]
. For completeness in this section we include all details of the reduction.

We first recall the following definition of a (not necessarily synchronous) two-prover strategy for a game
G
, which we immediately specialize to the case of the tensor code test.

Definition 4.5
.

A
two-prover strategy

\mathscr{S}
for the tensor code test is a tuple
({|\psi\rangle},A,B,P,\tilde{A},\tilde{B},\tilde{P})
where
{|\psi\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
is a state in the tensor product of two finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
, and
A,B,P
(resp.
\tilde{A},\tilde{B},\tilde{P}
) are the following families of projective measurements:

1.

Points measurements
: for every
u\in[n]^{m}
, let
A^{u}=\{A^{u}_{a}\}_{a\in\Sigma}
(resp.
\tilde{A}^{u}=\{\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\}_{a\in\Sigma}
)
denote the first (resp. second) prover’s measurement corresponding to the points question
u
.

2.

Lines measurements
: for every axis-parallel line
\ell\subset[n]^{m}
, let
B^{\ell}=\{B^{\ell}_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{C}}
(resp.
\tilde{B}^{\ell}=\{\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{C}}
) denote the first (resp. second) prover’s measurement corresponding to the lines question
\ell
.

3.

Pair measurements
: for every subcube
H
and for every
u,v\in H
, let
P^{u,v}=\{P^{u,v}_{a,b}\}_{a,b\in\Sigma}
(resp.
\tilde{P}^{u,v}=\{\tilde{P}^{u,v}_{a,b}\}_{a,b\in\Sigma}
) denote the first (resp. second) prover’s measurement corresponding to the points pair question
(u,v)
.

This definition is the same as Definition
3.1
given at the start of Section
3
, except that we have decoupled the measurement operators associated with each prover and the tracial state
\tau
has been replaced by the state
{|\psi\rangle}
, with the additional tensor product structure. (There is a more general class of strategies where all prover operators act on the same possibly infinite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
but all operators associated with one prover are required to commute with the other prover’s operators; we do not consider such
commuting-operator
strategies here.)

Because we do not assume a priori that the provers must employ the same measurement operators we modify the tensor code test by adding a “synchronicity test’ described in Figure
2
. To distinguish this test from the original tensor code test we refer to it as “the two-prover tensor code test.”

With probably
\tfrac{1}{3}
each, perform either of the two tests described in Figure
1
, where the roles of “prover
A
” and “prover
B
” are attributed at random (under the constraint that each prover gets assigned a different role). With the remaining probability
\frac{1}{3}
perform the following test.

3.

Synchronicity test:

Let
\ell\subset[n]^{m}
be a line sampled according to the same distribution as in the axis-parallel line test, i.e.
\ell
is a uniformly random axis-parallel line. Let
(u,v)
be a pair of points sampled according to the same distribution as in the commutation test, i.e.
u
and
v
are both uniformly distributed in the same random subcube. With probability
\frac{1}{3}
each, send either
\ell
, or
u
, or the pair
(u,v)
, to both provers. Accept if and only if the provers’ answers are identical.

Figure 2:
The two-prover tensor code test.

The following is analogous to Definition
3.2
.

Definition 4.6
.

We say that
\mathscr{S}
is an
(\varepsilon,\delta,\xi)
-good strategy for the two-prover tensor code test if the following hold:

1.

(Consistency between points and lines:)

The strategy passes the axis-parallel line test with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
. Formally,

\E_{\ell\subset[n]^{m}}\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a\in\Sigma}\,\frac{1}{2}\Big({\langle\psi|}B^{\ell}_{[g\mapsto g(u)|a]}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}{|\psi\rangle}+{\langle\psi|}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{[g\mapsto g(u)|a]}{|\psi\rangle}\Big)\,\geq\,1-\varepsilon\;,

where the expectation is over a uniformly random axis-parallel line
\ell\subset[n]^{m}
and a uniformly random point
u\in\ell
.

2.

(Consistency between points and pairs:)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\\
u,v\sim H\end{subarray}}\sum_{a,b\in\Sigma}\,\frac{1}{2}\Big({\langle\psi|}P^{u,v}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a|a]}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}{|\psi\rangle}+{\langle\psi|}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{P}^{u,v}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a|a]}{|\psi\rangle}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\,1-\delta\;,

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\\
u,v\sim H\end{subarray}}\sum_{a,b\in\Sigma}\,\frac{1}{2}\Big({\langle\psi|}P^{u,v}_{[(a,b)\mapsto b|b]}\otimes\tilde{A}^{v}_{b}{|\psi\rangle}+{\langle\psi|}A^{v}_{b}\otimes\tilde{P}^{u,v}_{[(a,b)\mapsto b|b]}{|\psi\rangle}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\,1-\delta\;,

where the expectation is over a uniformly random subcube
H=H_{x_{m-j+2},\ldots,x_{m}}
(sampled by choosing a random
j\sim[m]
and random
x_{m-j+2},\ldots,x_{m}\sim[n]
) and uniformly random points
u,v\sim H
.

3.

(Synchronicity:)

\frac{1}{3}\Big(\E_{u\sim[n]^{m}}\sum_{a\in\Sigma}\,{\langle\psi|}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}{|\psi\rangle}+\E_{\ell\subset[n]^{m}}\sum_{f}{\langle\psi|}B^{\ell}_{f}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f}{|\psi\rangle}+\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\\
u,v\sim H\end{subarray}}\sum_{a,b\in\Sigma}{\langle\psi|}P^{u,v}_{a,b}\otimes\tilde{P}^{u,v}_{a,b}{|\psi\rangle}\Big)\,\geq\,1-\xi\;,

where the expectations are uniform over a point
u
and an axis-parallel line
\ell
, and as in the previous item for
(H,u,v)
.

Finally, we say that a two-prover strategy
passes the (two-prover) tensor code test with probability
1-\varepsilon
if the strategy is
(\varepsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
-good for some
\varepsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}
such that
\frac{1}{3}(\varepsilon^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon
.

We can now state our two-prover extension of Theorem
4.1
.

Theorem 4.7
.

There exists a function

\eta^{\prime}(m,t,k,\varepsilon,n^{-1})=\mathrm{poly}(m,t,k)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,n^{-1},e^{-k/m^{2}})

such that the following holds.
Let
\mathscr{S}=({|\psi\rangle},A,B,P,\tilde{A},\tilde{B},\tilde{P})
be a two-prover strategy that passes the two-prover tensor code test for
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
with probability
1-\varepsilon
, where
{|\psi\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
, and
k\geq 12mt
. Then there exists projective measurements
\{G_{c}\}_{c\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}
and
\{\tilde{G}_{c}\}_{c\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
respectively such that the following hold, on expectation over a uniformly random
u\sim[n]^{m}
and a uniformly random axis-parallel line
\ell\subset[n]^{m}
:

\displaystyle A^{u}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\;,

(23)

\displaystyle G_{[c\mapsto c|_{\ell}\mid f]}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f}\;,

(24)

\displaystyle G_{c}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}_{c}\;,

(25)

where
\eta^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})
with
t=n-d+1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{S}=({|\psi\rangle},A,B,P,\tilde{A},\tilde{B},\tilde{P})
be as in the statement of the theorem.
The proof proceeds in three steps. The first and main step consists in applying
[
Vid21
, Corollary 4.1]
to Theorem
4.1
to obtain the following.

Claim 4.8
.

There exists measurements
\{G_{c}\}
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\{\tilde{G}_{c}\}
on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that

\displaystyle A^{u}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{1}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\;,

(26)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{1}}{G}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

(27)

for some
\eta_{1}=\eta_{1}(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,n^{-1})
.

Proof.

To apply
[
Vid21
, Corollary 4.1]
we first need to construct a
symmetric
strategy, which means that
{|\psi\rangle}
can be written as

{|\psi\rangle}\,=\,\sum_{i}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}{|u_{i}\rangle}\otimes{|u_{i}\rangle}\,\in\,\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}\;,

(28)

where the
\lambda_{i}
are non-negative and
\{{|u_{i}\rangle}\}
orthonormal in some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and the two provers’ measurement operators are identical up to a transpose:
\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}=(A^{u}_{a})^{T}
for all
u,a
, where the transpose is taken with respect to the basis
\{{|u_{i}\rangle}\}
, and similarly for the
B
and
P
measurement operators. The idea to achieve this is standard. First assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{H}_{A}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{B}\simeq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. This can always be achieved by enlarging
\mathcal{H}_{A}
or
\mathcal{H}_{B}
as needed. Next define

{|\psi^{\prime}\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big({|\psi\rangle}\otimes{|0\rangle}\otimes{|1\rangle}+(S{|\psi\rangle})\otimes{|1\rangle}\otimes{|0\rangle}\big)\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\;,

where
S
is the “SWAP” operator
S=(2P-I)
with
P
the projection on the symmetric subspace Span
\{{|u\rangle}\otimes{|u\rangle}:\,{|u\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}\}
. Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
. For any
u,a
define
(A^{\prime})^{u}_{a}=A^{u}_{a}\otimes{|0\rangle}\!{\langle 0|}+(\tilde{A}^{u}_{a})^{T}\otimes{|1\rangle}\!{\langle 1|}
and similarly
B^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
. Then (reordering the subsystems of
{|\psi^{\prime}\rangle}
in the obvious way so that
{|\psi^{\prime}\rangle}\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
) for any
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
among the newly defined measurement operators we have that

\displaystyle{\langle\psi^{\prime}|}X^{\prime}\otimes(Y^{\prime})^{T}{|\psi^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\big({\langle\psi|}X\otimes\tilde{Y}{|\psi\rangle}+{\langle\psi|}S^{\dagger}(\tilde{X}^{T}\otimes Y^{T})S{|\psi\rangle}\big)

\displaystyle={\langle\psi|}X\otimes\tilde{Y}{|\psi\rangle}\;.

As a consequence, the symmetric strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
also passes the tensor code test with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Next we introduce some notation associated to the application of the corollary.
Let
\mathcal{X}=[n]^{m}\cup([n]^{m-1}\times[m])\cup([n]^{m})^{2}
be the set of all questions that can be asked to the first prover in the two-prover tensor code test,
\mathcal{Y}=\{\bot\}
a singleton, and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
. Let
p
be the uniform distribution on
[n]^{m}\subset\mathcal{X}
, and for
u
in the support of
p
, let
g_{u}:\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}
map
a\in\Sigma
to the set of all
c\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
such that
c(u)=a
. Finally let
\kappa:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be the function
\kappa(\omega)=1-\eta^{\prime}(\omega)
, where
\eta^{\prime}
is defined as follows.
Let
\eta(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})
be the function from Theorem
4.1
. Let
m,t
and
n
be the parameters of the two-prover tensor code test, which we consider to be fixed. If
\eta
is a concave function of
\varepsilon
we can let
\eta^{\prime}(\omega)=\eta(m,t,\omega,n^{-1})
, with
m,t
and
n
fixed by the parameters for If
\eta
is not concave we proceed as follows. Since
\eta
has polynomial dependence on
\varepsilon
we can write it as a finite sum
\eta(\varepsilon)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}\varepsilon^{c_{i}}
where
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}(m,t,n^{-1})\in\mathbb{R}
and
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
are an increasing sequence of positive constants depending on the form of
\eta
only. Moreover, since
\eta(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,n^{-1})
then for all
i
,
|\alpha_{i}|=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n^{-1})
. Now for
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
we have that
\eta(\varepsilon)\leq\eta^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon)=\alpha\varepsilon^{c_{1}}
where
\alpha=\sum_{i}|\alpha_{i}|
and so
\alpha=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n^{-1})
. We then define
\kappa=1-\eta^{\prime\prime}
.

With this setting, Theorem
4.1
shows that the assumptions of
[
Vid21
, Corollary 4.1]
are satisfied, for the game
\mathfrak{G}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},\nu,D)
which is specified by the two-prover tensor code test. This is because (using terminology from
[
Vid21
]
) for any symmetric PME strategy
({|\psi\rangle},A)
there is a tracial strategy
(\tau,A)
for
\mathfrak{G}

that reproduces exactly the same distribution on answers in the game; for this it suffices to define the trace as
\tau(X)=\frac{1}{d}\mbox{\rm tr}(X)
for any operator
X
on the
d
-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}
that underlies the strategy
A
.

We apply the conclusion of
[
Vid21
, Corollary 4.1]
to the symmetric projective strategy
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
and hence
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
must be
(3\varepsilon,\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon,3\varepsilon)
-good according to Definition
4.6
, we deduce that in the terminology from
[
Vid21
]
,
\delta_{sync}(\mathscr{S}^{\prime};\nu)\leq 3\varepsilon
. As a consequence we obtain the existence of a measurement
\{G^{\prime}_{c}\}
on
\mathcal{H}
such that

\E_{u\sim[n]^{m}}\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}{\langle\psi^{\prime}|}(A^{\prime})^{u}_{c(u)}\otimes G^{\prime}_{c}{|\psi^{\prime}\rangle}\,\geq\,1-\eta^{\prime\prime}(m,t,\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon),n^{-1})\;,

(29)

where

\eta^{\prime\prime}(m,t,\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon),n^{-1})=\eta^{\prime}(m,t,\varepsilon+\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon),n^{-1})+\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon)=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,n^{-1})\;.

It remains to derive (
26
) and (
27
). Let
G_{c}=(I_{A}\otimes{\langle 0|})G^{\prime}_{c}(I_{A}\otimes{|0\rangle})
and
\tilde{G}_{c}=(I_{B}\otimes{\langle 1|})G^{\prime}_{c}(I_{B}\otimes{|1\rangle})
, where
I_{A}
(resp.
I_{B}
) denotes the projection on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{B}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
). Then
\{G_{c}\}
and
\{\tilde{G}_{c}\}
are measurements on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
respectively, and (
26
) and (
27
) follow directly from (
29
), with
\eta_{1}=2\eta^{\prime\prime}
, by expanding
{|\psi^{\prime}\rangle}
and
(A^{\prime})^{u}_{c(u)}
on the left-hand side using their definition.
∎

Claim
4.9
almost establishes (
23
), except that
\{\tilde{G}_{c}\}
is not necessarily a projective measurement. So in the second step we turn
\{G_{c}\}
and
\{G^{\prime}_{c}\}
into projective measurements.

Claim 4.9
.

There exists a projective measurement
\{G^{\prime}_{c}\}
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
(resp.
\{\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{c}\}
on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
) such that

\displaystyle A^{u}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{2}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\;,

(30)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{2}}{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

(31)

for some
\eta_{2}=\eta_{2}(m,t,\varepsilon,n^{-1})=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,n^{-1})
.

Proof.

We do the proof for
\{G_{c}\}
, as the proof for
\{\tilde{G}_{c}\}
is entirely analogous.
Write the Schmidt decomposition
{|\psi\rangle}=\sum_{i}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}{|u_{i}\rangle}{|v_{i}\rangle}
, where the
\lambda_{i}
are non-negative reals and
\{{|u_{i}\rangle}\}
(resp.
\{{|v_{i}\rangle}\}
) an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{B}
). Let
K=\sum_{i}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}{|u_{i}\rangle}{\langle v_{i}|}
. Let
G^{u}_{a}=\sum_{c:\,c(u)=a}G_{c}
. Then

\displaystyle 1-\eta_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{u}\sum_{c}{\langle\psi|}G_{c}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{c(u)}{|\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}{\langle\psi|}G^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}{|\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G^{u}_{a}K\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}K^{T}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G^{u}_{a}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G^{u}_{a}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\rho_{B}^{1/2}\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\rho_{B}^{1/2}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\;,

(32)

where the first inequality is (
26
) and the last inequality follows by Cauchy-Schwarz, and
\rho_{A}=KK^{\dagger}
and
\rho_{B}=K^{\dagger}K
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=\E_{u}\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G^{u}_{a}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G^{u}_{a}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)-\sum_{c\neq c^{\prime}}\E_{u}\sum_{a}1_{c(u)=c^{\prime}(u)=a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c^{\prime}}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\eta_{1})^{2}-\gamma_{m}\,\sum_{c\neq c^{\prime}}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c^{\prime}}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-2\eta_{1}-\gamma_{m}\;,

(33)

where the second line follows from (
32
) for the first term and Proposition
2.18
and
\mbox{\rm tr}(G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c^{\prime}}\rho_{A}^{1/2})\geq 0
for all
c,c^{\prime}
for the second. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

1-2\eta_{1}-\gamma_{m}\,\leq\,\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)\,\leq\,\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}^{2}\rho_{A}\big)\;.

We apply
Lemma

5.1
to the measurement
\{G_{c}\}
, with
\tau
defined by
\tau(X)=\mbox{\rm tr}(X\rho_{A})
. From the lemma it follows that there exists a projective measurement
\{G^{\prime}_{c}\}
such that

\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big((G_{c}-G^{\prime}_{c})^{2}\rho_{A}\big)\,=\,O\big(2\eta_{1}+\gamma_{m}\big)\;.

(34)

Then

\displaystyle\E_{u}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}{\langle\psi|}G^{\prime}_{c}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{c(u)}{|\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle=\E_{u}\sum_{c}{\langle\psi|}G_{c}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{c(u)}{|\psi\rangle}+\E_{u}\sum_{c}{\langle\psi|}(G^{\prime}_{c}-G_{c})\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{c(u)}{|\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\eta_{1}-\Big(\E_{u}\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(((G^{\prime})^{u}_{a}-G^{u}_{a})\rho_{A}^{1/2}((G^{\prime})^{u}_{a}-G^{u}_{a})\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\E_{u}\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\rho_{B}^{1/2}\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\rho_{B}^{1/2}\big)\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\eta_{1}-O\big(\eta_{1}+\gamma_{m}\big)^{1/4}\;,

where the first inequality uses (
27
) for the first term and Cauchy-Schwarz for the second, and the last is justified by the following:

\displaystyle\E_{u}\sum_{a}

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm tr}\big(((G^{\prime})^{u}_{a}-G^{u}_{a})\rho_{A}^{1/2}((G^{\prime})^{u}_{a}-G^{u}_{a})\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2-2\E_{u}\sum_{a}\mbox{\rm tr}\big((G^{\prime})^{u}_{a}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G^{u}_{a}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2-2\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G^{\prime}_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)+\gamma_{m}

\displaystyle=2-2\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big((G^{\prime}_{c}-G_{c})\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)+\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)+\gamma_{m}

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta_{1}+3\gamma_{m}+\Big(\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big((G^{\prime}_{c}-G_{c})^{2}\rho_{A}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_{c}\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G_{c}^{2}\rho_{A}\big)\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta_{1}+3\gamma_{m}+O\big(\sqrt{2\eta_{1}+\gamma_{m}}\big)\;,

where the second inequality uses that
\mbox{\rm tr}\big(G^{\prime}_{c}\rho_{A}^{1/2}G_{c^{\prime}}\rho_{A}^{1/2}\big)\geq 0
for all
c,c^{\prime}
and Proposition
2.18
, the fourth line uses (
33
), and the last uses (
34
). The claimed bound on
\eta_{2}
follows from the same bound on
\eta_{1}
and the bound on
\gamma_{m}
provided in Proposition
2.18
.
∎

With (
30
) we have established (
30
).
In the last step we obtain (
24
) and (
25
).

Claim 4.10
.

Let
\{G^{\prime}_{c}\}
and
\{\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{c}\}
be projective measurements that satisfy (
30
) and (
31
) respectively. Then

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c|_{\ell}\mid f]}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{3}}\mathbf{1}\otimes B^{\ell}_{f}\;,

(35)

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{c}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{3}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{c}\;,

(36)

for some
\eta_{3}=\mathrm{poly}(\eta_{2},(mt)/n,\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

We start with (
36
). Write

\displaystyle\sum_{c}G^{\prime}_{c}\otimes\tilde{G}_{c}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\gamma_{m}}\E_{u}\sum_{a}{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}

(37)

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta^{\prime}_{3}}\E_{u}\sum_{a}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}

(38)

\displaystyle\simeq_{3\xi}\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

(39)

where
\eta^{\prime}_{3}=2\sqrt{2\eta_{2}}
and recall that
\xi=3\varepsilon
. Each of the three approximations above is justified as follows. For (
37
), form the difference

\displaystyle\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\E_{u}1_{c(u)=c^{\prime}(u)}G^{\prime}_{c}\otimes\tilde{G}_{c^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{m}\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

using Proposition
2.18
. For (
38
), write

\displaystyle\E_{u}

\displaystyle\sum_{a}{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}-\E_{u}\sum_{a}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}

\displaystyle=\E_{u}\sum_{a}\big({G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\big)\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\big)+\E_{u}\sum_{a}\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\big)\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}-{A}^{u}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}\big)\;.

Each of the two terms above is bounded in the same way. We do it for the first:

\displaystyle\E_{u}

\displaystyle\sum_{a}{\langle\psi|}\big({G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\big)\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\big){|\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\E_{u}\sum_{a}{\langle\psi|}\big({G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{A}^{u}_{a}\big)^{2}{|\psi\rangle}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\E_{u}\sum_{a}{\langle\psi|}\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\big)^{2}{|\psi\rangle}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\eta_{2}}\;,

by (
31
) and the fact that
\{G^{\prime}\}
and
\{\tilde{A}\}
are families of projective measurements. Finally, (
39
) follows directly from the synchronicity condition.

To conclude we show (
35
).

\displaystyle\E_{\ell}\sum_{f}{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c|_{\ell}\mid f]}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\gamma_{m}}\E_{\ell}\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a}{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f\mapsto f(u)\mid a}

(40)

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta^{\prime\prime}_{3}}\E_{\ell}\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}

(41)

\displaystyle\simeq_{\eta_{2}}\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

(42)

where
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{3}=\sqrt{2\eta^{\prime}_{3}}+\sqrt{4\varepsilon}
.
We justify each of these three approximations as follows. For (
40
), form the difference

\E_{\ell}\sum_{f,f^{\prime}}\E_{u\in\ell}1_{f(u)=f^{\prime}(u)}G^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c|_{\ell}\mid f^{\prime}]}\otimes B^{\ell}_{f}\,\leq\,\gamma_{1}\,\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\,\leq\,\gamma_{m}\,\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;,

by Proposition
2.18
. For (
41
), write

\displaystyle\E_{\ell}

\displaystyle\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a}{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f\mapsto f(u)\mid a}-\E_{\ell}\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a}A^{u}_{a}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell}\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a}{\langle\psi|}\big({G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\otimes\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\big)\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f\mapsto f(u)\mid a}\big){|\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle\qquad+\E_{\ell}\E_{u\in\ell}\sum_{a}{\langle\psi|}\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{[c\mapsto c(u)\mid a]}\big)\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\tilde{B}^{\ell}_{f\mapsto f(u)\mid a}-A^{u}_{a}\otimes\mathbf{1}\big){|\psi\rangle}\;.

Each of the two terms above is bounded as previously, using (
37
) for the first and the consistency condition between points and lines for the second. This gives
\eta^{\prime\prime}_{3}=\sqrt{2(\gamma_{m}+\eta^{\prime}_{3}+3\xi)}+\sqrt{4\varepsilon}
. Finally, (
42
) is by (
30
). The claimed bound on
\eta_{3}
follows from Proposition
2.18
to bound
\gamma_{m}
and
\xi\leq 3\varepsilon
.
∎

The theorem follows using the bound on
\eta_{2}
given in Claim
4.9
.
∎

5
Self-improvement

In this section we prove
Lemma

4.2
. The proof of the lemma relies on three main ingredients.

Orthogonalization.

The first is a lemma that allows us to round a submeasurement that is self-consistent to a projective measurement. The lemma first appears in
[
KV11
]
for the finite-dimensional case, in
[
JNV+20b
]
with an improved error dependence and was extended to the infinite-dimensional case in
[
dlS21
]
. The proof uses the polar decomposition.

Lemma 5.1

(Orthogonalization)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a von Neumann algebra with a normal state
\tau
.
9
9

9

Even though in our application
\tau
will be tracial, this is not required for the lemma.

Let
A=\{A_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\subset\mathscr{A}
denote a submeasurement. Suppose that

\sum_{a}\tau(A_{a}(\mathbf{1}-A_{a}))\leq\zeta.

Then there exists a projective submeasurement
\{P_{a}\}\subset\mathscr{A}
such that

A_{a}\approx_{\sqrt{18\zeta}}P_{a}\;.

Proof.

We indicate how the lemma follows from
[
dlS21
, Theorem 1.2]
. Let
n=|\mathcal{A}|+1
and identify
\mathcal{A}
with the set
\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
. Let
A_{n}=\mathbf{1}-\sum_{a}A_{a}
so
\{A_{a}\}_{a\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}
is a measurement. Applying
[
dlS21
, Theorem 1.2]
, we get orthogonal projections
P_{a}
satisfying

A_{a}\approx_{\sqrt{9\delta}}P_{a}\;,

(43)

where

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{a=1}^{n}\tau\big(A_{a}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}\tau(A_{a}(\mathbf{1}-A_{a}))+\tau\big(A_{n}-A_{n}^{2}\big)\;,

(44)

where for the second line we used that
\{A_{a}\}_{a\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}
is a measurement. The first term is at most
\zeta
by assumption. For the second term we write

\displaystyle\tau\big(A_{n}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\tau\Bigg(A_{n}\bigg(\mathbf{1}-\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}A_{a}\bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\tau\big(A_{n})-\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}\tau\big(A_{n}A_{a}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\tau\big(A_{n})-\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}\tau\big(\big(\mathbf{1}-A_{a}\big)A_{a}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\tau\big(A_{n}\big)-\zeta\;,

where the inequality on the third line uses that

A_{n}\,=\,\mathbf{1}-\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}A_{a}\,\leq\,\mathbf{1}-A_{a}

for all
a\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, and
A_{a}\geq 0
for all
a
.
Thus (
44
) is at most
2\zeta
, which together with (
43
) proves the lemma.
∎

Complementary slackness.

The second ingredient is a duality result about positive linear maps which appears in
[
dlS21
]
. The proof is based on an application of the Hahn-Banach theorem.

Lemma 5.2

(Proposition 7.1 in
[
dlS21
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a von Neumann algebra
and let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}:\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}
be normal positive linear maps. Then there is a unique normal linear map
\psi:\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}
of minimal norm such that
\psi\geq\varphi_{i}
for all
i
. Moreover there exists a measurement
\{T_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
such that for all
X\in\mathscr{A}
,

\psi(X)=\sum_{i}\varphi_{i}(T_{i}X)

(45)

In finite dimensions this lemma corresponds to the complementary slackness condition of a particular semidefinite program satisfying strong duality (see
[
NV18b
, Lemma 10]
for the finite-dimensional version of this statement).
In the proof of
Lemma

4.2
, we use
Lemma

5.2
to define the “maximum”
\psi
of a collection of linear forms
\varphi_{i}
. This step can be thought of as a replacement for “majority decoding” in analyses of low-degree testing in the commutative (i.e. classical) case
[
GLR+91
,
RS92
]
.

Local-to-global variance bound.

The third ingredient is the following lemma. Recall that
\mathcal{C}
is a
[n,k,d]_{\Sigma}
code. Define
\gamma=1-\frac{d}{n}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(\tau,A,B,P)
be an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-good strategy for the tensor code test with
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
.
Let
\{T_{g}\}
be a measurement with outcomes in
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
. Then on average over uniform
x,y\in[n]^{m}
,

A^{x}_{g(x)}T^{1/2}_{g}\approx_{\zeta_{var}}A^{y}_{g(y)}T^{1/2}_{g}

where
\zeta_{var}=\sqrt{m}\,\Big(2\sqrt{2\varepsilon}+2\sqrt{\gamma}\Big)
.

Before proving
Lemma

5.3
we first prove a “local” version of
Lemma

5.3
, where the closeness of
A^{x}_{g(x)}T^{1/2}_{g}
and
A^{y}_{g(y)}T^{1/2}_{g}
hold when
x,y
are random points from a random axis-parallel line (rather than being random points from the entire domain
[n]^{m}
).

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(\tau,A,B,P)
be an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-good strategy for the tensor code test with
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
.
Then on overage over axis-parallel lines
\ell
, and
x,y\in\ell
,

A^{x}_{g(x)}T^{1/2}_{g}\approx_{\zeta_{local}}A^{y}_{g(y)}T^{1/2}_{g}

where
\zeta_{local}=2\sqrt{2\varepsilon}+2\sqrt{\gamma}
.

Proof.

By definition of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-good strategy we have
A^{x}_{a}\simeq_{\varepsilon}B^{\ell}_{[f\mapsto f(x)\mid a]}
, and thus by
Proposition

2.5
we have
A^{x}_{a}\approx_{\sqrt{2\varepsilon}}B^{\ell}_{[f\mapsto f(x)\mid a]}
. This implies that

A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}^{1/2}\approx_{\sqrt{2\varepsilon}}B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=g(x)]}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}

(46)

on average over a uniformly random axis-parallel line
\ell
and a uniformly random point
x\in\ell
. This holds because

\displaystyle\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\left\|\Big(A^{x}_{g(x)}-B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=g(x)]}\Big)T_{g}^{1/2}\right\|^{2}_{\tau}=\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{a\in\Sigma}\sum_{g:g(x)=a}\left\|\Big(A^{x}_{g(x)}-B^{\ell}_{[f\mapsto f(x)\mid a]}\Big)T_{g}^{1/2}\right\|^{2}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{a\in\Sigma}\sum_{g:g(x)=a}\tau\left(\Big(A^{x}_{a}-B^{\ell}_{[f\mapsto f(x)\mid a]}\Big)^{2}T_{g}\right)\leq\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{a\in\Sigma}\tau\Big(\Big(A^{x}_{a}-B^{\ell}_{[f\mapsto f(x)\mid a]}\Big)^{2}\Big)\leq 2\varepsilon

where the first inequality follows from the fact that
\{T_{g}\}
is a measurement. Next, we have that

B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=g(x)]}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma}}B^{\ell}_{g|_{\ell}}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}~.

(47)

This holds because

\displaystyle\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\left\|\Big(B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=g(x)]}-B^{\ell}_{g|_{\ell}}\Big)T_{g}^{1/2}\right\|^{2}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{C}}\tau\left(\Big(B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=h(x)]}-B^{\ell}_{h}\Big)^{2}T_{[g:g|_{\ell}=h]}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell,x\in\ell}\sum_{h\in\mathcal{C}}\tau\left(B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=h(x),f\neq h]}\cdot T_{[g:g|_{\ell}=h]}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell}\sum_{h,f\in\mathcal{C}}\Big(\E_{x\in\ell}\mathbb{I}[f(x)=h(x),f\neq h]\Big)\cdot\tau\left(B^{\ell}_{f}\cdot T_{[g:g|_{\ell}=h]}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\E_{\ell}\sum_{h,f\in\mathcal{C}}\gamma\cdot\tau\left(B^{\ell}_{f}\cdot T_{[g:g|_{\ell}=h]}\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma

where the third line follows from the fact that the
\{B^{\ell}_{h}\}
measurements are projective, in the fourth line we used
\mathbb{I}[f(x)=h(x),f\neq h]
to denote the characteristic function of the indicated set, the fifth line follows from the fact that two distinct codewords
f\neq h
can only agree on at most
\gamma
fraction of coordinates, and the last line follows from the fact that
\{B^{\ell}_{f}\}
and
\{T_{g}\}
are measurements. Thus putting together (
46
) and (
47
) we get

\displaystyle A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}^{1/2}\approx_{\sqrt{2\varepsilon}}B^{\ell}_{[f:f(x)=g(x)]}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma}}B^{\ell}_{g|_{\ell}}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\gamma}}B^{\ell}_{[f:f(y)=g(y)]}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\approx_{\sqrt{2\varepsilon}}A^{y}_{g(y)}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}.

on average over a random axis-parallel line
\ell
and independent random points
x,y\in\ell
.
∎

The proof of
Lemma

5.3
now follows by using the expansion of the hypercube to transfer the “local variance” bound of
Lemma

5.4
to a “global variance” bound.

Proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

Define the graph
G
to have vertex set
[n]^{m}
, and vertices

x,y\in[n]^{m}
are connected if and only if they differ in at most one
coordinate.
Define
N=n^{m}
.
The Laplacian
L
associated with this graph
G
has a zero eigenvector

{|\varphi_{0}\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in[n]^{m}}{|x\rangle}.

Furthermore the second largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}
is equal to
\frac{1}{mN}
.

For all
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
, define the positive linear functional

\psi_{g}(X)=\tau\big(T_{g}^{1/2}\cdot X\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\big)
.
By
Lemma

A.2
, we have that

\displaystyle\E_{x,y\sim{[n]}^{m}}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\left\|\bigl(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)}\bigr)T_{g}^{1/2}\right\|_{\tau}^{2}

\displaystyle=\E_{x,y\sim[n]^{m}}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\psi_{g}\left(\Big(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)}\Big)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq m\E_{\ell,x,y\in\ell}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\psi_{g}\left(\Big(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)}\Big)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=m\,\E_{(x,y)\sim G}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}}\left\|\bigl(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)}\bigr)T_{g}^{1/2}\right\|_{\tau}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq m\,\zeta_{local}^{2},

which completes the proof by definition.
∎

We now proceed to prove
Lemma

4.2
.

5.1
Proof of
Lemma

4.2
, Step 1: Construction of (non-projective)
H

Let
A=\{A^{x}\}_{x\in[n]^{m}}
and
\{G_{g}\}
be as in the statement of
Lemma

4.2
. Let
\mathscr{A}
and
\tau
be the underlying von Neumann algebra and normal trace. For all
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
define

A_{g}=\E_{x}A^{x}_{g(x)}\;,

where the expectation is uniform over all
x\in[n]^{m}
.
For all
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
define
\varphi_{g}:\mathscr{A}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\forall X\in\mathscr{A}\;,\qquad\varphi_{g}(X)=\tau(X\cdot A_{g})\;.

Notice that
\varphi_{g}
is a normal positive linear map. It is positive because
\tau(XA_{g})=\tau(A_{g}^{1/2}XA_{g}^{1/2})\geq 0
for positive
X\in\mathscr{A}
. It is normal because
\tau
is normal, which by definition (see
Section

2
) implies that there is a positive trace class operator
M
such that
\tau(XA_{g})=\tau(A_{g}^{1/2}XA_{g}^{1/2})=\mbox{\rm tr}(A_{g}^{1/2}XA_{g}^{1/2}M)
. Since
XA_{g}^{1/2}M
is a trace class operator and
A_{g}^{1/2}
is bounded, we have that
\mbox{\rm tr}(A_{g}^{1/2}XA_{g}^{1/2}M)=\mbox{\rm tr}(XA_{g}^{1/2}MA_{g}^{1/2})
; this implies that
\varphi_{g}(X)=\mbox{\rm tr}(XA_{g}^{1/2}MA_{g}^{1/2})
is normal because
A_{g}^{1/2}MA_{g}^{1/2}
is a positive trace class operator.

By
Lemma

5.2
there exists a unique normal linear map
\psi
of minimal norm satisfying
\psi\geq\varphi_{g}
for all
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
. Moreover there exists a measurement
\{T_{g}\}
such that

\forall X\in\mathscr{A}\;,\qquad\psi(X)=\sum_{g}\varphi_{g}(T_{g}X)\;.

(48)

We refer to
Equation

48
as the
complementary slackness
condition.

For all
x\in[n]^{m}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
let

H^{x}_{g}=A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\;.

We verify that for all
x\in[n]^{m}
,
\{H^{x}_{g}\}
forms a submeasurement:

\displaystyle\sum_{g}H^{x}_{g}=\sum_{g}A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}=\sum_{a}A^{x}_{a}\Big(\sum_{g:g(x)=a}T_{g}\Big)A^{x}_{a}\leq\sum_{a}(A^{x}_{a})^{2}=\sum_{a}A^{x}_{a}=\mathbf{1}.

(49)

Finally, for
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
define

H_{g}=\E_{x\sim[n]^{m}}H^{x}_{g}\;.

We proceed to argue that, except for the projectivity condition, the submeasurement
\{H_{g}\}
satisfies the conclusions of
Lemma

4.2
. We first show a technical lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\zeta_{var}=\sqrt{m}(2\sqrt{2\varepsilon}+2\sqrt{\gamma})
as in

Lemma

5.3
.
Then

\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\big(\big|H_{g}-A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}A^{x}_{g(x)}\big|\big)\leq{2\,\zeta_{var}}\;.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\big(\big|H_{g}-A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}A^{x}_{g(x)}\big|\big)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\Big(\Big|\E_{y}\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)T_{g}A^{y}_{g(y)}+\E_{y}A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)\Big|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\E_{x,y}\sum_{g}\tau\big(\big|\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)T_{g}A^{y}_{g(y)}\big|\big)+\tau\big(\big|A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)\big|\big)

\displaystyle\leq\E_{x,y}\sum_{g}\Big(\big\|\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)T_{g}^{1/2}\big\|_{\tau}\cdot\big\|T_{g}^{1/2}A^{y}_{g(y)}\big\|_{\tau}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\big\|A^{x}_{g(x)}T_{g}^{1/2}\big\|_{\tau}\cdot\big\|T_{g}^{1/2}\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)\big\|_{\tau}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(\E_{x,y}\sum_{g}\big\|\big(A^{y}_{g(y)}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)T_{g}^{1/2}\big\|_{\tau}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\E_{x}\sum_{g}\big\|T_{g}^{1/2}A^{x}_{g(x)}\big\|_{\tau}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\;,

where the second line uses the triangle inequality for the
1
-norm
(
Item

4
of
Proposition

2.1
), the third the Hölder
inequality (
Item

2
of
Proposition

2.1
), and the fourth the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
The lemma then follows from Lemma
5.3
together with the fact that

\|A^{x}_{a}\|\leq 1
for every
x,a
and
\sum_{g}T_{g}\leq\mathbf{1}
.
∎

5.1.1
Bounding the completeness of
H

Lemma 5.6
.

\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g})\geq 1-\nu
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g})

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\right)

(50)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\right)

Cyclicity of trace, projectivity of
A^{x}_{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}\tau\left(T_{g}\cdot A_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}\varphi_{g}(T_{g})

Definition of
\varphi_{g}

\displaystyle=\psi(\mathbf{1})

Complementary slackness

(51)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}\psi(G_{g})

\{G_{g}\}
is a measurement

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{g}\varphi_{g}(G_{g})

\psi\geq\varphi_{g}
for all
g

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}\tau(G_{g}\cdot A_{g})

Definition of
\varphi_{g}

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau(G_{[g(x)=a]}\cdot A^{x}_{a})

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau(G_{[g(x)=a]})-\E_{x}\sum_{b\neq a}\tau(G_{[g(x)=a]}\cdot A^{x}_{b})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\nu\;,

by the assumption that
\{G_{g}\}
is
\nu
-inconsistent with
A
.
∎

5.1.2
Bounding the consistency of
H
with
A

Let
\zeta_{var}
be as in Lemma
5.5
.

Lemma 5.7
.

On average over
x\sim[n]^{m}
,
H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\simeq_{2\,{\zeta_{var}}}A^{x}_{a}
.

Proof.

By expanding the definition of
H
we get

\displaystyle\E_{x}\sum_{a\neq b}\tau\left(A^{x}_{b}\cdot H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g,b:g(x)\neq b}\tau\left(A^{x}_{b}H_{g}\right)

(52)

\displaystyle\approx_{2\,{\zeta_{var}}}\E_{x}\sum_{g,b:g(x)\neq b}\tau\left(A^{x}_{b}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\right)

(53)

\displaystyle=0

Projectivity of
A^{x}_{b}

(54)

Here the application of Lemma
5.5
is justified by forming the difference between (
53
) and (
52
) and bounding it as

\displaystyle\big|\eqref{eq:si-cons-1b}-\eqref{eq:si-cons-1a}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\E_{x}\Big|\sum_{g,b:g(x)\neq b}\tau\big(A^{x}_{b}\cdot(H_{g}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)\big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\E_{x}\sum_{g}\big|\tau\big((\mathbf{1}-A^{x}_{g(x)})\cdot(H_{g}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\big)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\Big(\big|H_{g}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\big|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq{2\,{\zeta_{var}}}\;,

where the third line follows from Hölder’s inequality (
Item

3
of
Proposition

2.1
) and
\|\mathbf{1}-A^{x}_{g(x)}\|\leq 1
.
∎

5.1.3
Bounding the projectivity of
H

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\gamma_{m}=1-(d/n)^{m}
. Then

\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}-H_{g}^{2})\leq 5\,{\zeta_{var}}+\gamma_{m}
.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}^{2})

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(H^{x}_{g}\cdot H_{g}\right)

\displaystyle\approx_{2\,{\zeta_{var}}}\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot H^{x}_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)\;,

(55)

where the approximation on the second line follows from
Lemma

5.5
. Now we write

RHS of
Eq.

55

\displaystyle\approx_{\gamma_{m}}\E_{x}\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)

To be proved below

(56)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)

A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}=H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}

(57)

\displaystyle\approx_{\,{\zeta_{var}}}\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{y}_{g(y)}\cdot T_{g}\right)

To be proved below

(58)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(H_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A_{g}\cdot T_{g}\right)

(59)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\varphi_{g}(T_{g}\cdot H_{g^{\prime}})

Definition of
\varphi_{g}

(60)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g^{\prime}}\psi(H_{g^{\prime}})

Complementary slackness

(61)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{g^{\prime}}\varphi_{g^{\prime}}(H_{g^{\prime}})

\psi\geq\varphi_{g^{\prime}}
for all
g^{\prime}

(62)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g^{\prime}}\tau(H_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A_{g^{\prime}})

Definition of
\varphi_{g^{\prime}}

(63)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau\left(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot A^{x}_{a}\right)

(64)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau\Big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot\Big(\mathbf{1}-\sum_{b\neq a}A^{x}_{b}\Big)\Big)

(65)

\displaystyle=\tau(H)-\E_{x}\sum_{a\neq b}\tau\big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot A^{x}_{b}\big)

(66)

\displaystyle\geq\tau(H)-2\,{\zeta_{var}}

(67)

This completes the proof of the lemma, modulo Eq. (
56
) and Eq. (
58
), which we now turn to.

Proof of approximation in
Eq.

56
.

We aim to show that

\Big|\E_{x}\sum_{g^{\prime}\neq g}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)\Big|\leq\gamma_{m}\;.

To see this, we show

\displaystyle\E_{x}\sum_{g^{\prime}\neq g}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g\neq g^{\prime}}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot A^{x}_{g^{\prime}(x)}\cdot T_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g^{\prime}(x)}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g\neq g^{\prime}}\tau\left(A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}[g(x)=g^{\prime}(x)]

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g\neq g^{\prime}}\tau\left(T_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}[g(x)=g^{\prime}(x)]

Cyclicity of trace

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{g\neq g^{\prime}}\tau\left(T_{g^{\prime}}\cdot H_{g}\right)\cdot\mathbb{I}[g(x)=g^{\prime}(x)]~.

Definition of
H_{g}

(68)

To conclude, note that since
\tau(T_{g^{\prime}}H_{g})\geq 0
for all
g,g^{\prime}
and
\sum_{g\neq g^{\prime}}\tau(T_{g^{\prime}}H_{g})\leq 1
it follows that (
68
) is at most

\max_{g\neq g^{\prime}}\;\E_{x}\,\mathbb{I}[g(x)=g^{\prime}(x)]\,\leq\,1-\frac{d^{m}}{n^{m}}=\gamma_{m}\;,

because two distinct codewords
g\neq g^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
can only agree on at most
n^{m}-d^{m}
coordinates.

Proof of approximation in
Eq.

58
.

We aim to show that

\E_{x}\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{x}_{g(x)}\cdot T_{g}\right)\approx_{\,{\zeta_{var}}}\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot A^{y}_{g(y)}\cdot T_{g}\right)\;.

(69)

To do this, we bound the magnitude of the difference:

\displaystyle\Big|\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,g^{\prime}}\tau\left(H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\cdot(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)})\cdot T_{g}\right)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\E_{x,y}\sum_{g}\tau\Big(T_{g}^{1/2}\cdot\Big(\sum_{g^{\prime}}H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\Big)\cdot(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)})\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\Big(T_{g}^{1/2}\cdot\Big(\sum_{g^{\prime}}H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\Big)^{2}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\Big)}\cdot\sqrt{\E_{x,y}\sum_{g}\tau\Big(T_{g}^{1/2}\cdot\big(A^{x}_{g(x)}-A^{y}_{g(y)}\big)^{2}\cdot T_{g}^{1/2}\Big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{1}\cdot\,{\zeta_{var}}

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz, the fact that
\{H^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\}
and
\{T_{g}\}
are (sub)measurements, and
Lemma

5.3
.
This concludes the proof of (
67
) and hence the lemma.
∎

5.2
Proof of
Lemma

4.2
, Step 2: Making
H
projective

To conclude the proof of
Lemma

4.2
, we show how to modify the submeasurement
\{H_{g}\}
defined and analyzed in the previous section so that it is projective. Recall that according to Lemma
5.8

\{H_{g}\}
satisfies

\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}-H_{g}^{2})\,\leq\,\eta\;,

for
\eta=5\,{\zeta_{var}}+\gamma_{m}
. This condition allows us to apply the Orthogonalization Lemma (
Lemma

5.1
). From the lemma it follows that there exists a projective submeasurement
H^{\prime}=\{H_{g}^{\prime}\}
satisfying

H_{g}\approx_{\sqrt{18\eta}}H_{g}^{\prime}\;.

5.2.1
Proof of
Item

1
(Completeness)

The completeness of the projective submeasurement
\{H_{g}^{\prime}\}
is related to the completeness of
\{H_{g}\}
in the following manner:

\displaystyle\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot H_{g}^{\prime})

\{H_{g}^{\prime}\}
is projective

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{18\eta}}\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}\cdot H_{g}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{18\eta}}\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}\cdot H_{g})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g})-\eta

Lemma
5.8

(70)

5.2.2
Proof of
Item

2
(Consistency with
A
)

Next we show consistency of
\{H_{g}^{\prime}\}
with the measurements
\{A^{x}_{a}\}
. Towards this we first prove that for all
x
,

H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\approx_{\eta^{\prime}}H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\;,

where

\eta^{\prime}=\sqrt{2\sqrt{18\eta}+\eta}.

This is because

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\tau\big((H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}-H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{2})+\sum_{a}\tau((H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime})^{2})-2\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]})+\sum_{a}\tau((H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}))-2\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g})+\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}^{\prime})-2\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g})+\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}^{\prime})-2\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}\cdot H_{g}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{18\eta}+\eta.

The third line follows from the fact that
\{H_{[g(x)=a]}\}
and
\{H_{[g(x)=a]}^{\prime}\}
are submeasurements. The fifth line follows from dropping terms
\tau(H_{g}\cdot H_{g^{\prime}}^{\prime})
for
g\neq g^{\prime}
, which are nonnegative. The sixth line follows from (each step of) the derivation (
70
).

Using the fact that
\{A^{x}_{a}\}
forms a complete measurement,

\displaystyle\E_{x}\sum_{a\neq b}\tau\Big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\cdot A^{x}_{b}\Big)

\displaystyle=\tau(H^{\prime})-\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau\Big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\cdot A^{x}_{a}\Big)

\displaystyle\approx_{\eta^{\prime}}\tau(H^{\prime})-\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau\Big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot A^{x}_{a}\Big)

\displaystyle\approx_{\eta^{\prime}}\tau(H)-\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau\Big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot A^{x}_{a}\Big)

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{a\neq b}\tau\Big(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}\cdot A^{x}_{b}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\,{\zeta_{var}}

(71)

where the second line follows from
Proposition

2.7
, the third line follows from (
70
), and the last line follows from Lemma
5.7
.

5.2.3
Proof of
Item

3
(Agreement is well-explained)

The map
\psi
constructed at the start of Section
5.1
satisfies
\psi(X)\geq\varphi_{g}(X)=\tau(X\cdot A_{g})
for all
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
and positive
X\in\mathrm{B}(\mathcal{H})
. We compute

\displaystyle\psi(\mathbf{1}-H^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\psi(\mathbf{1})-\psi(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\psi(\mathbf{1})-\sum_{g}\psi(H_{g}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\psi(\mathbf{1})-\sum_{g}\tau(H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot A_{g})

\displaystyle=\psi(\mathbf{1})-\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\cdot A^{x}_{a})

\displaystyle=\tau(H)-\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\cdot A^{x}_{a})

\displaystyle\approx_{\eta^{\prime}}\tau(H^{\prime})-\E_{x}\sum_{a}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\cdot A^{x}_{a})

\displaystyle=\E_{x}\sum_{a\neq b}\tau(H_{[g\mapsto g(x)\mid a]}^{\prime}\cdot A^{x}_{b})

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta^{\prime}+2\,{\zeta_{var}}

where the equality
\psi(\mathbf{1})=\tau(H)
follows from the equality between (
50
) and (
51
), the sixth line follows from (
70
), and the last line follows from (
71
).
This implies

\psi(\mathbf{1}-H^{\prime})\leq 3\eta^{\prime}+2\,\zeta_{var}.

5.2.4
Putting everything together

To conclude the proof of
Lemma

4.2
, we set

\zeta=3\eta^{\prime}+2\,{\zeta_{var}}\;.

First we note that, since
t=n-d+1
, we can express
\gamma=1-\frac{d}{n}\leq\frac{t}{n}
and

\gamma_{m}=1-\frac{d^{m}}{n^{m}}=1-\Big(1-\frac{t-1}{n}\Big)^{m}\leq 1-\Big(1-\frac{mt}{n}\Big)=\frac{mt}{n}.

By the definitions of
\eta^{\prime}
and
\zeta_{var}
, we have

\displaystyle\zeta_{var}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m}(2\sqrt{2\varepsilon}+2\sqrt{\gamma})=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}\left(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{n}\right)~,

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=5\zeta_{var}+\gamma_{m}=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}\left(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{n}\right),

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\sqrt{18\eta}+\eta}=\mathrm{poly}(\eta)=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}\left(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{n}\right),

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=3\eta^{\prime}+2\zeta_{var}=\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\mathrm{poly}\left(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{n}\right).

6
Pasting

We now prove
Lemma

4.3
.
For convenience we restate the lemma here:

See
4.3

6.1
Commutativity of the
A
’s

We first derive a consequence of passing the subcube commutation test with high probability.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=(\tau,A,B,P)
be strategy for the tensor code test with
code
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes(m+1)}
that passes the subcube commutation test with probability
1-\delta
. Let
A=\{A^{u,x}\}_{u\in[n]^{m},x\in[n]}
denote the points measurements.
On average over uniform and independently random
(u,x),(v,y)\sim[n]^{m}\times[n]
,

A^{u,x}_{a}A^{v,y}_{b}\approx_{\sqrt{32(m+1)\delta}}A^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a}.

Proof.

Suppose the strategy
\mathscr{S}
passes the subcube commutation test with probability
1-\delta
. Since the index
j=1
in the test (see
Figure

1
)
with probability
(m+1)^{-1}
, the strategy passes the test conditioned on
j=1
with probability at least
1-\delta(m+1)
. When
j=1
, the “subcube” is
actually the entire space
[n]^{m+1}
.
This means that

A^{u,x}_{a}\simeq_{(m+1)\delta}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a]},

on average over
(u,x),(v,y)\in[n]^{m+1}
which implies

A^{u,x}_{a}\approx_{\sqrt{2(m+1)\delta}}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a]},

(72)

by
Proposition

2.5
.

By multiple applications of
Proposition

2.8
, we have

\displaystyle A^{u,x}_{a}A^{v,y}_{b}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{2(m+1)\delta}}A^{u,x}_{a}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto b]}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{2(m+1)\delta}}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a]}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto b]}

\displaystyle=P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto b]}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a]}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{2(m+1)\delta}}A^{v,y}_{b}P^{u,x,v,y}_{[(a,b)\mapsto a]}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{2(m+1)\delta}}A^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a}.

The theorem now follows from the triangle inequality.
∎

6.2
Commutativity of the
G
’s

Having deduced that the points measurements approximately commute on average, we now aim to deduce that the “subspace measurements”
\{G^{x}\}
corresponding to the parallel
m
-dimensional subspaces
S_{x}=\{(u,x):u\in[n]^{m}\}
approximately commute. We first prove a slightly weaker statement, which is that the subspace measurements
\{G^{x}\}
give rise to points measurements
\{G^{u,x}_{a}\}_{a\in\Sigma}
that approximately commute.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\nu_{1}=8(\sqrt{\zeta}+\sqrt{(m+1)\delta})
.
Let
\{G^{x}_{g}\}
be projective submeasurements satisfying the conditions in

Lemma

4.3
.
Define
G^{u,x}_{a}=G^{x}_{[g\mapsto g(u)\mid a]}
for all
(u,x)\in{[n]}^{m}\times[n]
.
Then on average over uniform and independently uniform
(u,x)
,

(v,y)\sim{[n]}^{m}\times[n]
,

G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}\approx_{\nu_{1}}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}~.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(\Bigl(G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}-G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\Bigr)^{*}\Bigl(G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}-G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\Bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=2

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}-G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right)

where in the second line we used the cyclicity of the trace
\tau
and the
fact that
\{G^{u,x}_{a}\}
is projective.
By the consistency assumption (Assumption
2
of

Lemma

4.3
), we have
G^{u,x}_{a}\simeq_{\zeta}A^{u,x}_{a}
and
therefore by
Proposition

2.10
we have, on average over

(u,x)\sim{[n]}^{m+1}
,

\displaystyle G^{u,x}_{a}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\zeta}}G^{u,x}_{a}A^{u,x}_{a}

(73)

\displaystyle G^{u,x}_{a}

\displaystyle\approx_{2\sqrt{\zeta}}G^{x}A^{u,x}_{a}

(74)

where
G^{x}=\sum_{a}G^{x}_{[g\mapsto g(u)\mid a]}
.

The rest of the proof is outlined in the following sequence of approximations.

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle\approx_{2\sqrt{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{x}A^{u,x}_{a}\right)

To be proved below

(75)

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a}\right)

To be proved below

(76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}\right)

Cyclicity of the trace

\displaystyle\approx_{2\sqrt{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{y}A^{v,y}_{b}\right)

To be proved below

(77)

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}A^{v,y}_{b}\right)

To be proved below

(78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}A^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a}\right)

Cyclicity of the trace

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{32(m+1)\delta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}A^{u,x}_{a}A^{v,y}_{b}\right)

Lemma

6.1
, Prop.
2.7

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}A^{v,y}_{b}\right)

To be proved below

(79)

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right).

To be proved below

(80)

This shows that

\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right)\approx_{8\sqrt{\zeta}+\sqrt{32(m+1)\delta}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right).

Proof of approximation in
Eqs.

75
and
77
.

Define
G^{u,x,v,y}_{a}=\sum_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}
and it
is easy to verify that
\{G^{u,x,v,y}_{a}\}
forms a submeasurement.
Hence, we have

\begin{split}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right)&=_{\phantom{2\sqrt{\zeta}}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a}\tau\left(G^{u,x,v,y}_{a}\cdot G^{u,x}_{a}\right)\\
&\approx_{2\sqrt{\zeta}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a}\tau\left(G^{u,x,v,y}_{a}\cdot G^{x}A^{u,x}_{a}\right)\\
&=_{\phantom{2\sqrt{\zeta}}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{x}A^{u,x}_{a}\right),\end{split}

where the approximation follows from

Propositions

2.7
and
74
.
This gives
Eq.

75
.

Similarly, to prove the approximation in
Eq.

77
, we
define
H^{u,x,v,y}_{b}=\sum_{a}A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}
.
Then

\begin{split}\sum_{b}H^{u,x,v,y}_{b}{\bigl(H^{u,x,v,y}_{b}\bigr)}^{*}&=\sum_{b}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}}A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{u,x}_{a^{\prime}}G^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a^{\prime}}\\
&=\sum_{b}\sum_{a}A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a}\\
&\leq\sum_{a,b}A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}A^{u,x}_{a}\\
&\leq\mathbf{1},\end{split}

where the second line uses the projectivity of
\bigl\{G^{u,x}_{a}\bigr\}

and the third line uses
G^{u,x}_{a}\leq\mathbf{1}
.
By
Propositions

2.7
and
74
, we have

\begin{split}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}\right)&=_{\phantom{2\sqrt{\zeta}}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{b}\tau\left(H^{u,x,v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}\right)\\
&\approx_{2\sqrt{\zeta}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{b}\tau\left(H^{u,x,v,y}_{b}G^{y}A^{v,y}_{b}\right)\\
&=_{\phantom{2\sqrt{\zeta}}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{y}A^{v,y}_{b}\right).\end{split}

Proof of approximations in
Eqs.

76
and
78
.

Let
R^{x}_{g}=\E_{v,y}\sum_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{x}_{g}G^{v,y}_{b}
.
It can be verified that
\{R^{x}_{g}\}
forms a submeasurement:

\sum_{g}R^{x}_{g}\leq\E_{v,y}\sum_{b}{(G^{v,y}_{b})}^{2}\leq\mathbf{1}.

To show the approximation in
Eq.

76
, we bound the
magnitude of the difference:

\displaystyle\left|\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{{a,b}}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}G^{v,y}_{b}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot A^{u,x}_{a}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\E_{u,x}\sum_{a}\tau\left(R^{x}_{[g\mapsto g(u)\mid a]}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot A^{u,x}_{a}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\E_{u,x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(R^{x}_{g}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot A^{u,x}_{g(u)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq_{\phantom{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(R^{x}_{g}\right)}\cdot\sqrt{\E_{u,x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{g(u)}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot R^{x}_{g}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot A^{u,x}_{g(u)}\right)}

Cauchy-Schwarz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\E_{u,x}\sum_{g}\tau\left(A^{u,x}_{g(u)}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot R^{x}_{g}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot A^{u,x}_{g(u)}\right)}

\{R^{x}_{g}\}
is a submeasurement

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\E_{x}\sum_{g}\tau\left((\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot R^{x}_{g}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot\E_{u}A^{u,x}_{g(u)}\right)}

\{A^{x}_{a}\}
is projective

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\E_{x}\sum_{g}\psi^{x}\left((\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\cdot R^{x}_{g}\cdot(\mathbf{1}-G^{x})\right)}

Assumption
3

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\E_{x}\psi^{x}\left(\mathbf{1}-G^{x}\right)}

\{R^{x}_{g}\}
is submeasurement,
\{G^{x}\}
is projective

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\zeta}

\displaystyle\quad\text{Assumption~\ref{enu:pasting-boundedness}}.

The proof of approximation in
Eq.

78
follows in an
identical manner.

Proof of approximations in
Eqs.

79
and
80
.

To show the approximation in
Eq.

79
, we define

S^{v,y}=\sum_{b}A^{v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b},

and verify that

S^{v,y}{(S^{v,y})}^{*}=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}A^{v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b^{\prime}}A^{b,y}_{b^{\prime}}=\sum_{b}A^{v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}A^{b,y}_{b}\leq\mathbf{1},

and

\sum_{a}S^{v,y}G^{u,x}_{a}{(S^{v,y}G^{u,x}_{a})}^{*}\leq S^{v,y}{(S^{v,y})}^{*}\leq\mathbf{1}.

Applying
Proposition

2.7
to
Eq.

73
, we
have

\begin{split}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}A^{u,x}_{a}\right)&=_{\phantom{\sqrt{\zeta}}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a}\tau\left(S^{v,y}G^{u,x}_{a}\cdot G^{u,x}_{a}A^{u,x}_{a}\right)\\
&\approx_{\sqrt{\zeta}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a}\tau\left(S^{v,y}G^{u,x}_{a}\cdot G^{u,x}_{a}\right)\\
&=_{\phantom{\sqrt{\zeta}}}\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(A^{v,y}_{b}G^{v,y}_{b}G^{u,x}_{a}\right),\end{split}

which proves
Eq.

79
by cyclicity of the trace.

The proof of approximation in
Eq.

80
follows in an
identical manner.

∎

We now show that the subspace measurements
\{G^{x}\}
themselves approximately commute.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\gamma_{m}
be as defined in Proposition
2.18
and
\nu_{2}=4(\gamma_{m}+\nu_{1})
. Let
\{G^{x}_{g}\}
be projective submeasurements satisfying the conditions in

Lemma

4.3
.

Then on average over independently uniform
x,y\sim[n]
,

G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\approx_{\nu_{2}}G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}.

Proof.

By definition, we need to bound

\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,h}\tau\left({\Big(G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}-G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\Big)}^{*}\Big(G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}-G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\Big)\right)=2\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,h}\tau\left(G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}-G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\right)

where we used the cyclicity of the trace
\tau
and the projectivity of
\{G^{x}_{g}\}
.

For notational convenience we use the abbreviation

G^{u,x}_{a}=G^{x}_{[g\mapsto g(u)\mid a]}
for all

(u,x)\in{[n]}^{m}\times[n]
.
We have

\displaystyle\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,h}\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\right)

\displaystyle\approx_{\gamma_{m}\phantom{m}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),y}\sum_{a,h}\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\right)

To be proved below

(81)

\displaystyle\approx_{\gamma_{m}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\,G^{v,y}_{b}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\right)

To be proved below

(82)

\displaystyle\approx_{\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\,G^{v,y}_{b}\right)

Propositions

2.7
and
6.2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{a,b}\tau\left(G^{v,y}_{b}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\right)

Projectivity of
G
’s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{x,y}\sum_{g,h}\tau\left(G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\right).

Proof of approximation in
Eq.

81
.

To show the approximation in
Eq.

81
, we bound the magnitude of the
difference:

\displaystyle\left|\E_{(u,x),y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,g^{\prime},h:\\
g\neq g^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\,\mathbb{I}[g(u)=g^{\prime}(u)]\,\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=_{\phantom{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\E_{x,y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,g^{\prime},h:\\
g\neq g^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\right)\,\E_{u}\mathbb{I}[g(u)=g^{\prime}(u)]

\tau(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g^{\prime}})\geq 0

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\E_{x,y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,g^{\prime},h:\\
g\neq g^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\right)\,\Big(1-\frac{d^{m}}{n^{m}}\Big)

Proposition
2.18

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}

where in the last inequality we used that summing
\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g}\,G^{y}_{h}\,G^{x}_{g^{\prime}}\right)
over
g,g^{\prime},h
is at most
1
.

Proof of approximation in
Eq.

82
.

To show the approximation in
Eq.

82
, we bound the magnitude of the difference:

\displaystyle\left|\E_{(u,x),(v,y)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,h,h^{\prime}:\\
h\neq h^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\,\mathbb{I}[h(v)=h^{\prime}(v)]\,\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\,G^{y}_{h^{\prime}}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=_{\phantom{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\E_{(u,x),y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,h,h^{\prime}:\\
h\neq h^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\tau\left(G^{y}_{h}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\,G^{y}_{h^{\prime}}\,G^{u,x}_{a}\right)\,\E_{v}\mathbb{I}[h(v)=h^{\prime}(v)]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\gamma_{m}\;.

∎

6.3
Pasting the
G
’s together: Method 1

Now that we have established the subspace submeasurements
\{G^{x}\}
approximately commute, we “paste” them together into a single submeasurement
H=\{H_{h}\}
with outcomes in
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes(m+1)}
that are consistent with the
G
’s. We give two methods for achieving this. The first, detailed in this section, is the simpler method, but it achieves a weaker bound: instead of the value of
\mu
claimed in
Lemma

4.3
this method obtains

\mu(\kappa,m,t,\varepsilon,\delta,\zeta,n^{-1})=\kappa+\mathrm{poly}(m,t)\cdot\left(\mathrm{poly}\left(\varepsilon,\delta,\zeta,n^{-1}\right)\right)^{1/t}\;,

(83)

with a dependence on
1/t
in the exponent.
Intuitively, for this method we define
H
as a measurement that tries to
“simultaneously” measure
t=n-d+1
subspace submeasurements

G^{x_{1}},\ldots,G^{x_{t}}
for a randomly chosen tuple of distinct coordinates

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})\in[n]^{t}
, obtain
m
-dimensional words

g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m}
, and then interpolate a “global”
codeword
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes m+1}
such that
h(u,x_{i})=g_{i}(u)
.
Since the base code
\mathcal{C}
is interpolable, by
Proposition

2.20
,
there is always a global codeword
h
consistent with
g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}
.

While the exponential dependence on
1/t
in (
83
) remains acceptable for applications where
t
is thought of as constant, if the parameter grows even e.g. logarithmically with
n
the bound can become trivial. To address this case in Section
6.4
we give a different method (explained in that section), which obtains the bound claimed in
Lemma

4.3
.

We now formalize the first “interpolation” approach. We start by precisely defining the pasted submeasurement
H
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\in[n]
.
